  about four molecules per million a year, measured since 1989; a hundredth of the stock in five centuries).
- **Andrey corrects article 13** (and the comment under 14): "the oxygen will vanish very quickly" and the boiling
  oceans are taken back. Arithmetic: about 1.2 million billion tonnes of oxygen in the air; burning everything alive
  takes about a thousandth, all extractable coal, oil and gas a percent or two; with nothing green the stock lasts
  millions of years. Kept: "none of the new ones needs this stock, so none of them will guard it".
- The debt of 07 is paid (the reader under 07: nature is the only thing truly exploited; the promised link of the
  economy to ecology): **the economy is the metabolism of the technosphere; ecology is what that metabolism does to
  the environment of the form of life before it; one flow counted from two banks.** "Which nature do we defend?":
  21% oxygen, fifteen degrees, fresh water, "our list"; nobody defends the swamp bacteria ("we boil the jars").
  Andrey does not call on anyone to stop: every form of life defends its environment; "the planet does not need
  saving; it has seen worse, and afterwards the worse was called progress".
- What the technosphere breathes: chips are born in rooms about a thousand times cleaner than an operating theatre,
  protected from people, the finest steps in a vacuum or nitrogen; the sealed cylinder of servers in dry nitrogen on
  the sea bottom failed eight times less often than on land (no oxygen, no damp, no people: "a jar of mushrooms the
  other way round"); a data centre near the Arctic Circle; what it breathes out is heat. It needs energy and cold.
  The author's image of the cosmonaut (70 kg of meat and bones and several hundred tonnes of life support) is used:
  "a piece of the old air, as a spore carries its shell". The author's 2012 forecast (techno-life will adapt the
  environment to itself more actively than man did, not out of malice; the unneeded is "junk") is quoted and marked
  "my forecast, not a fact".
- Oleg's objections and what Andrey concedes: (1) it took the bacteria a billion years: the pace was set by the
  makers of the waste and by the sinks; carbon dioxide went from 315 to over 425 parts per million in one lifetime;
  "a factory does not wait for a mutation, it is copied" (the term "two clocks" of 32 is not used). (2) We understand
  and can agree (the ozone hole): accepted as the best case, with the reason it worked (a substitute at hand, a few
  plants, "no want was cut"; the shield itself is "made of the dump"); where a want must be cut, emissions grew by
  more than half since the 1992 treaty; a firm's net-zero pledge "moved after the arithmetic; nobody lied".
  "Pull the plug" is deferred to its own evening (51). (3) Let it go to space (a reader under 11): it will (29), but
  "childhood is spent where you were born". (4) The anaerobes are alive, so where is the gloom: "'they live in
  niches' is not a consolation, it is the forecast". (5) Who counted the dead: nobody; no grave has been found; the
  dying is deduced from what oxygen does today to those without guards.
- Strengths stated: solid (the rust, the 2 and the 30, the gut bacteria, the carbon dioxide record, the cylinder); a
  hypothesis that is not Andrey's (oxygen brought the ice); Andrey's without proof (chimneys and data centres are the
  breath of a new form of life and not simply our dirt; the unneeded will not be guarded); open (what happens to
  those in the way: "more than one road, and the niche is only the first").
- Nobody says the forbidden word; sixth tick "again".

**Images and devices used**: the jar of pickled mushrooms (the swollen lid, the vinegar, "a flat lid proves nothing",
"I'll boil them, to spite the old tenants"); "Breathe, breathe. It is waste"; the stock-keeper and the full warehouse;
the bucket with a hole; the August pond; the factory that throws waste over the fence; fire kept in a stove; the axe
made of the first dump; the ledger's other column and "the same loaf"; the school book by the grandchildren of the
winners; the stick poked into the pond bottom; the walking jar of mushrooms; the pencil pointed at the smoke; "whose
lungs"; the nursery of chips; the jar the other way round; the cosmonaut as a spore; the shield made of the dump;
"Gloomy. I came to breathe. — Then breathe. There is enough for millions of years, I've counted. — Is that
objective? — To a thousandth."

**Facts used**: all from the writer's memory, with web search kept to link checks and one figure (at the keeper's
request); **check before reusing**. Lovelock (asked by NASA in the 1960s how to detect life on Mars; the atmosphere
as the sign); first forests about 385 million years ago; the Great Oxidation Event about 2.4 billion years ago;
about half of oxygen production in the ocean, the commonest marine cyanobacterium (Prochlorococcus) described in the
1980s; chloroplasts as swallowed cyanobacteria; anoxygenic photosynthesis on hydrogen sulphide and iron;
*Clostridium botulinum* and *C. tetani* as obligate anaerobes; catalase; retinopathy of prematurity (about 10,000
babies blinded in the 1940s and early 1950s); banded iron formations as the source of most iron ore, and the dispute
over how much of them free oxygen made; detrital pyrite before and red beds after; the Huronian glaciations and the
methane hypothesis (Kopp et al. 2005); 2 and about 30 molecules of ATP per glucose (textbooks: 30–32, older 36–38);
the brain: 2% of body weight, 20% of oxygen; Sender, Fuchs & Milo 2016 (3.0·10¹³ human cells, 3.8·10¹³ bacteria,
about 0.2 kg); about a kilogram of carbon dioxide breathed out per person a day (8.2 billion people: about 3 billion
tonnes a year); Global Carbon Budget 2024 (37.4 billion tonnes from fossil fuels and cement; about 22.7 in 1990);
the Scripps oxygen record since 1989 (about 4 ppm a year); the mass of atmospheric oxygen (1.2·10¹⁸ kg); the carbon
of all biomass (about 550 billion tonnes: Bar-On, Phillips & Milo 2018); Mauna Loa (315 ppm in 1958, above 425 now);
the Montreal Protocol (1987, ratified by every country; recovery by about 2040, over the Antarctic by about 2066:
WMO 2022); the UN climate convention (1992); Google (net zero by 2030 pledged in 2020; emissions up 48% from 2019 to
2023, report of 2024); clean rooms; Project Natick (checked against Wikipedia: 864 servers, deployed off Orkney on 1
June 2018, two years under water, inactive by 2024; from memory: dry nitrogen, one eighth of the failure rate on
land); the data centre in Luleå (2013); the ISS as "several hundred tonnes". The author's sources: the comment under
07 (ecology), the SETI-talk comments of 2012 (techno-life and "junk"; 70 kg of meat and bones), article 13 (methane
and vanishing oxygen), the comment under 14, the reader under 11 (it has no reason to stay on the Earth). Not used:
Margulis's phrase "oxygen holocaust"; the Carboniferous oxygen peak; sulphur isotopes; the water use of data
centres; the "boring billion" by name.

**Figures**: `oxygen-two-steps` (a scheme, not data), `two-and-thirty` (rounded textbook figures), `whose-breath`
(rough arithmetic and the Global Carbon Budget 2024).

**Left for later**: who swallowed whom (chloroplasts, mitochondria) → 42; the other fates of former masters and the
table → 48, 52 (38 gives the row "a niche"); "pull the plug: count who hangs on that cord" → 51; the technosphere
leaving for space was only pointed at (29); what the heir will do with the environment it does not need was left as
Andrey's forecast, without dates; Oleg's next topic, page three of the notebook, "why dreams come true" ("Has one
come true for you? — Not for me. My grandmother's did, all the time. — Then sleep attentively tonight") → 39.

## 39. Oleg's Prophetic Dream and the Price of a False Alarm

**Established**
- The answer to "why dreams come true" (33, 38). Oleg slept "attentively", dreamt of Genka, a classmate unseen for
  thirty years, and Genka phoned at nine in the morning: "Explain that, determinist." Andrey's first question: "And if
  he had not called, would you have told me this dream?"
- The arithmetic, on Oleg's own figures "taken from the ceiling": about 11,000 nights in thirty years; about 300
  people known once and lost; one of them turns up about five times a year (one day in seventy); a dream of one of
  them is remembered once a month (360 dreams). One dream: 1/70 × 1/300 = one in 21,000; 360 dreams: **about one
  chance in sixty** in thirty years. Oleg takes it as a win ("I wouldn't put money on such a horse"), and Andrey
  agrees that it is rare. Three widenings: the window is really a week (one in eight); the story would have been
  brought if it had happened to any of some forty people (about five stories in thirty years, and Oleg counts about
  that many); and the strict version across more than a hundred million adults gives **about 150 people every
  morning**. Littlewood's law is given in one sentence. The figures "give the order and no more".
- The dreams that did not come true cannot be named: they were written off in the stocktaking of 33, and the one that
  matched got a weight for life (the significance coefficient of 19, not named). "Your ledger has an income column
  and no expense column. Not because you are a dishonest bookkeeper. The warehouse is built that way."
- The birthday problem (Oleg's class of thirty: he says "under a tenth", it is seven in ten; 435 pairs; 23 people
  are enough for more likely than not). Anthropocentrism exposed in the small: "I counted from myself"; the first
  number is about you, the second about the world, "the marvel lives in the gap between them". Oleg: "anthropocentrism
  in a pocket edition".
- The determinist's answer (17): Genka is gathering the class for forty-five years since school; the round date has
  sat in two heads since the winter: **a common cause** ("two cocks crow at dawn together, and neither has woken the
  other"). Oleg had gone to bed waiting for a dream, or he would not have remembered it. "Chance or not?" is badly
  posed: for Andrey nothing is by chance, "chance" names the causes he cannot trace; the real question is whether
  there is a short road from the dream to the call or only the long one through the common past. One short road was
  found (the date); a road straight from the dream to the call Andrey does not see, "a fact about me, not about the
  road". Whether tomorrow can be remembered is deferred (59).
- The grandmother's dream book (raw meat, a tooth with blood, fish; a family of forty; no term; "prayed it away"): a
  forecast with no date and no refuting outcome "can't help coming true". Andrey's dated marks of 27–30 are named as
  the opposite, "for that very reason".
- Why we are built so. The kitchen smoke detector that screams at every cutlet: two mistakes, **a false alarm and a
  miss**, and one screw between them; it is set by the prices. The four squares in the ash (wind or snake, jumped or
  stayed): if a life is worth 100,000 minutes, it pays to jump when one rustle in 10,000 is a snake. Nesse's smoke
  detector principle; Foster and Kokko's formula. "We descend from those who jumped at the wind. Those who waited for
  proof were right more often, and left fewer children." The scream before the proof is the watchdog of 18, "barking
  before the scales have even been brought".
- Omens are the same screw with a lesson at stake: Oleg's tinned sprats of 1985 and the rat's taste aversion. **To
  learn is to link what came together before you know why**; whoever links at the first coincidence learns fast and
  sometimes knocks on wood. Skinner's eight pigeons (six with rituals) and the niece's "Freebie, come!" with the
  record book out of the window ("those who failed shouted badly"). The 1971 dispute over Skinner's reading is given.
- Oleg's strongest objection ("practice stands above theory; this happened to me; your probabilities are an excuse
  that can't be checked") is **not argued with**: the arithmetic proves only that a coincidence is enough, so the
  dream proves nothing in either direction. Andrey tells his own case from the author's novella (ch. 13): guessing
  cards with a friend, an apprentice electrician (up to nine in ten eye to eye, six in ten from the next room,
  nothing with two other friends), then the test with Zener cards and the long black shadow that slid from the
  window, crossed the floor and went under the door, tested with a frying pan (it had to be held against the wall
  inside the room; its shadow was pale and blurred; a shadow climbs up a door and does not go under it; about four
  metres long, "a skittle moving knob first"). "And what was it? — I don't know."
- **The probabilities are the continuation's, not the novella's**: for the cards, seven in ten on "the horse and the
  two cocks" (Clever Hans cues eye to eye; alike habits of choice if the card was not drawn by lot, "I wouldn't
  swear to it now"; the count kept by the two who wanted it to work), two on a generous count, one for what he cannot
  name; for the shadow, one in twenty that it was a shadow of something outside, and beyond that "my list of
  candidates is empty". No label is hung on it, "neither miracle nor nonsense". "Practice is what can be repeated, or
  at least was written down before the outcome. One case told afterwards is a recollection. Mine too."
- The method: the notebook by the bed, "Dreams. Before the phone", counted in a year. "Is this the whole of science?
  A notebook where the dreams that didn't come true are kept? — The greater part of it. Written before, checked
  after, counted all."
- The machine is brought in by Oleg after the middle (the chatbot gave him the same arithmetic "without a shiver": "a
  mind without a grandmother"). Andrey: the horse classifier that learned the archive's stamp ("its black cat"; "a
  horse again"), the flu forecast fitted from fifty million phrases to about a thousand points ("my three hundred
  classmates and eleven thousand nights, only without a conscience"). Whether the machine fears anything is **not
  claimed**; it has prices: a false link that fits the exercise book is paid like a true one, and at the exams "I
  don't know" gets nought while a guess sometimes gets a point ("the snake and the minute"; Oleg's invented book of
  33). "This time the prices were set by us, not by the grass" (22). The row for the table of 46: the task (on
  incomplete data, to choose which mistake to make more often) / our way (fear, the dream book, knocking on wood).
  What the heir's way will be is not known; it can keep all 360 dreams and count them, so it will be less
  superstitious where somebody examines it in a new place and more where nobody does. "Who will examine it when we
  no longer can?" is deferred.
- Oleg turns the weapon on Andrey: "One feeder, seven dances. How are you better than the pigeon?" Andrey: "the best
  question of this spring. I may be no better"; the pigeon does not write down the times the food did not come, and
  the marks lie in Oleg's notebook, written before the outcome; "whether that is enough we shall see". The question
  goes on the last page of the notebook → 66.
- Strengths stated: solid (the birthday pairs, the detector's screw, the stamp, the flu counted twice over); argued
  about (what the pigeon links); Oleg's (the three hundred, the one in seventy, the 150 every morning); Andrey's
  without proof (our omens and the machine's stamps are one and the same tilt); open (the shadow, and Genka "until
  the notebook is full"). "I had one miracle, and you divided it by sixty. — I left it to you. One in sixty is a rare
  thing. I only said that it isn't a message."
- Nobody says the forbidden word; seventh tick "again".

**Images and devices used**: the frying pan with potatoes (put on the coals, held up between the fire and the spruce
to cast a shadow that "climbs the trunk", put back at the end); Genka, the corridor and the ruler; "I wouldn't put
money on such a horse"; the wife's aunt and the telegram, the neighbour's burnt bathhouse; the other fires; "a
miracle a month, on schedule"; the ledger with no expense column; Lyuda and Vovka, 12 April; 435 tickets; "the marvel
lives in the gap"; two cocks at dawn; the short road and the long road; "till Thursday", "prayed it away"; "Freebie,
come!"; the cutlet and the towel; knuckles raised over the log; the four squares in the ash; "no more entries in the
ledger"; sprats in tomato sauce; "a superstition of the stomach"; "a pigeon, a rat and my grandmother"; the list with
an empty line; "Dreams. Before the phone"; "a mind without a grandmother"; the black cat in the corner of a
photograph; the exercise book and the exam; "one feeder, seven dances"; "To within the ceiling the numbers were taken
from."

**Facts used**: from the writer's memory, with web checks kept to the links and to four pages (at the keeper's
request); **check before reusing**. Checked on the day of writing: Littlewood's law as retold by F. Dyson (one event a
second for eight hours a day, about a million a month); the Hans Commission of thirteen (Stumpf; a circus manager,
the zoo director); Skinner 1948 (the rituals as listed); Lapuschkin et al. 2019, Nature Communications 10:1096 (a
Fisher-vector classifier on PASCAL VOC 2007 relied on a source tag present in about a fifth of the horse images;
removing it removed the "horse", pasting it on a car made a horse); Google Flu Trends (twice the CDC's doctor visits
in the 2012–13 season); Kalai, Nachum, Vempala and Zhang, "Why Language Models Hallucinate" (arXiv 2509.04664,
September 2025). From memory: the birthday figures are computed exactly in `tools/figures.py` (50.7% at 23, 70.6% at
30, 99% at 57; 7.6% for one's own birthday at 30); Nesse's smoke detector principle (2001, 2005); Foster & Kokko
2009 (Proc. R. Soc. B 276); Garcia's taste aversion in rats (1955, 1966: long delays, X-rays); Skinner's eight
pigeons at 15-second intervals, six with rituals; Staddon & Simmelhag 1971; Pfungst 1907 (Hans right about 89% of
the time when the questioner knew the answer and about 6% when not: given as "about nine in ten" and "hardly ever";
**the 89% and 6% were not found on the Wikipedia page, check**); Vasiliev (Leningrad physiologist, books on
telepathy); the five Zener symbols; Google Flu Trends (2008; 50 million search terms against 1,152 data points;
school basketball terms weeded out by hand: Lazer et al., Science 2014). The author's case: the novella
*Podpolshchiki*, ch. 13 (the cards: up to 90% eye to eye, 60% from the next room, no link with two other friends;
the shadow and the frying pan); the friend is not named. Invented for the frame (Oleg's side, not the author's):
Genka, the forty-five years since school, the niece's ritual, the sprats, the grandmother's dream book. Not used:
the voices of ch. 11 and the 95% / 70% estimate; the pneumonia X-ray network that learned the hospital (Zech et al.
2018; drafted and cut); the husky and the snow; pareidolia; the hot hand; Heider and Simmel (it belongs to 50).

**Figures**: `dreams-and-one-call` (a scheme of Oleg's count), `shared-birthday` (an exact count),
`price-of-two-mistakes` (a scheme).

**Left for later**: why we see a somebody behind every movement → 50 (not touched); whether tomorrow can be
remembered → 59 ("a separate evening"); "how are you better than the pigeon", on the last page of the notebook → 66;
who will examine the machine "when we no longer can" → the evenings on the heir's knowledge (60); the row
"superstition" of the table → 46; Oleg's notebook of dreams, to be counted in a year (a prop for the evenings of
2028); Oleg's next question, from Genka's news: "the plant is closed and the town stands; how is that?" (Andrey:
"count for tomorrow how many times a mouse's heart beats in its life, and an elephant's") → 40.

## 40. Why Firms Die and Cities Do Not

**Established**
- The answer to Oleg's question of 39 ("the plant is closed and the town stands; how is that?") and to the homework:
  by Oleg's count a mouse (25 g, 600 beats a minute, two and a half years) gets about 0.8 billion heartbeats and an
  elephant (5 t, 30 a minute, 65 years) about a billion; Oleg himself (70 a minute, 62 years old) has spent 2.3
  billion and "by the mouse's reckoning ran out at twenty-seven" ("thirty-five years on credit. — Nobody has asked
  for it back so far"). People and bats are named as exceptions; the rule holds "give or take a factor of two".
- The descent goes through the firewood: kindling burns faster than a log of the same weight because it has more
  surface per kilogram; "the mouse is kindling, the elephant is a log" (Rubner's surface law, 1883). The catch is in
  the numbers: by the skin an elephant should need about 3,500 times more food than a mouse, and it needs about
  10,000 (Kleiber's line: ten thousand times heavier, a thousand times more energy, not five hundred). A gram of
  elephant spends twenty times less than a gram of mouse and lives twenty times more slowly: "one clock run at
  different speeds; an elephant is a mouse played back slowly". The watts per kilogram of 35 are continued (a mouse
  about 8, an elephant under 0.5).
- West, Brown and Enquist: an animal is "not a bag" but a network of pipes whose last pipes (capillaries) are the same
  in a mouse and an elephant ("the same tap; the riser is different"). **Stated as argued about**: whether it is
  exactly three quarters and whether the pipes are the cause.
- Growth stops from inside: income comes through the pipes and lags behind the weight, upkeep does not; where they
  meet, growth ends. Oleg recognises the life-cycle curve of 10. Andrey adds to his own article: in 2011 he named
  ceilings that come from outside (the market, the food, the height to which blood can be lifted); this ceiling grows
  from inside, "the profit per turnover shrinks with every kilogram gained". First phrase to remember: **what grows
  more slowly than its own size**.
- "Of which system?": a town is not its people (half have left or died, new ones came), not its houses and not its
  plant, but what stays while they flow through it, "as water through the cells in the pot" (35) and atoms through a
  body. "A town is what remains when the people change. — And when the plants change." Oleg's town is three hundred
  years old and the plant lasted seventy.
- The Santa Fe count of cities: pipes (petrol stations, cables, roads) grow more slowly than the city (about an
  eighth less per inhabitant at each doubling), a person's own needs (housing, jobs, water) in step with it, and what
  happens between people (wages, patents, research jobs, and equally serious crime and new AIDS cases) faster: about
  15% more per inhabitant at each doubling. The explanation given is pairs (the 435 pairs of 39; sixty people make
  1,770): twice the people, four times the pairs, held back by streets and the hours of the day. What runs in the
  second network is the images of 19: "they breed where heads meet". Bigger animal, slower heart; bigger town, faster
  ("an elephant with a mouse's pulse"); the faster heart belongs to another system, the one that runs over the pipes.
  Second phrase to remember: **what grows faster than its own size**.
- Firms: they grow like animals (West's book, weighed as such), and of more than 25,000 traded North American
  companies half are gone in about ten years whatever the trade; the chance of dying does not depend on age. Andrey
  **does not stretch it**: a firm does not die of wear like an elephant, "it dies like a glass in the kitchen", and
  the animal part is only the growth that stops (10: what has stopped growing waits for its turn). "Died" mostly
  means bought or merged: "not buried but swallowed" (what becomes of the swallowed is deferred to 42). Towns are
  dropped too (the miners' settlement on one pit, "half a firm", one well); Oleg's town had four wells (the junction,
  the college, the market, the river). No town is known to have died of age.
- The mountain is brought in by Oleg, after the middle: Genka told him that the plant's foundry now holds racks of
  servers ("thick walls, its own substation, the river for cooling; a watchman and two engineers"). The lock with 37:
  what was built passes to the next owner at a discount; "the town is everything that was built, taken together, and
  the firms are tenants. — And so are the people." A forum reader's 2016 line is paraphrased without a name: towns
  are not where people live but the habitat of machines. Oleg: "too sharply; a town was built by people, for people.
  — Who goes in at the gates of your foundry now? — Electricity."
- The equation, counted as a column: gain proportional to size gives a geometric progression (13: the rate of profit
  is the exponent), a doubling every forty years (an example); with 15% more per head at each doubling every next
  doubling takes an eighth less (40, 35, 30, 26.5, 23…), and the whole infinite column sums to a little over three
  hundred years ("Achilles and the tortoise"). Panov's epochs of 29 are named as the same kind of column. Nothing real
  flies to infinity (13): before the wall growth stops and turns down, unless an invention moves the wall, and then
  the next one is needed sooner than the last ("a squirrel in a wheel, and the wheel speeds up by itself").
- The forbidden word: Andrey says only that physicists have a word for the point where a formula goes to infinity on
  a finite date and that it is printed in the paper "as an ordinary term, like boiling point"; Oleg, sure that it is
  being dragged in by the ears, reads it out and goes for firewood (see the frame).
- Who makes the inventions "sooner every time"? In the paper, people. Oleg's doubt (he learned for fifteen years, his
  great-nephew will learn till thirty) is deferred to 53; Oleg himself says what Andrey would say ("some who learn in
  a month and are copied in a minute; they are sitting in my foundry").
- Von Foerster's 1960 "Doomsday: Friday, 13 November, A.D. 2026" (the same kind of line for the number of people over
  two thousand years; a line of the author's 2011 table in 13). The date "was last autumn", and nothing happened:
  the line left that road long before (the largest yearly addition, 93 million, came in 1989, as in 26). "Infinity
  was called off; it always is." What was not called off is the fork: inventions sooner each time, or growth stops;
  "the number of heads has made its choice". World output grows by about three percent a year, an even geometric
  progression, "no speeding wheel" (conceded to Oleg): each step was paid for by putting more heads to inventing
  (deferred to 53); when the heads run out, either the steady trot ends too or somebody other than the heads makes
  the next steps. **Andrey's belief, stated as unproved**: the tenants of the foundry.
- Oleg's strongest objection is conceded in three parts: (1) the fifteen percent depends on where the edge of a town
  is drawn (England and Wales, thousands of boundaries: most measures proportional, the exponent "jumped about with
  the edge"; "in England, no steady one"); (2) perhaps the nimble simply move to big cities (Sweden: about half of the
  excess); (3) the line is a photograph across many towns in one year, not a film of one town, and the authors read
  their equation as a warning, not as a sentence (Andrey's gloss of "to sustain growth and avoid stagnation or
  collapse"). What is left: the sign (the animal slows with size, the town speeds up);
  that anything above "proportional" gives a finite sum (with a twentieth instead of an eighth, 800 years); and that
  the drawing of 13 (a wall on a finite date, and before it a stop or a change of law) was reached "from a mouse, a
  petrol station and a stopwatch on a pavement". "Two bell towers, one view. — Or two pigeons at one feeder" (the
  question of 39, already on the last page).
- Strengths stated: solid (a gram of elephant lives many times more slowly; half of the traded firms gone in ten
  years; large towns richer per head and faster on foot wherever a country was measured in one year); argued about
  (three quarters and the pipes; the fifteen percent, the edge, the newcomers); a hypothesis of its authors (the
  equation with the wall and the wheel); Andrey's (the technosphere is of the second kind, and the next jumps will
  not be made by heads).
- Eighth tick "again": "Came with a closed plant. Came out at the servers in its foundry, brought them in myself, and
  went for firewood on top of it."

**Images and devices used**: the log split into kindling (a handful thrown into the fire flares up); the first entry
in the notebook of dreams (a tram, somebody's dog, the late foreman and the quarterly report; nobody rang); "I
thought I had dropped a nought"; living on credit; the mouse as kindling and the elephant as a log; "a mouse played
back slowly"; the tap and the riser; the puppy's income and expenses; the guppy, the hen and the cow on one curve;
the town's streets, station, market "on the same square as in my grandmother's day", the bridge; running up a moving
escalator; "an elephant with a mouse's pulse"; wages, patents and burglaries in one basket; news, a rumour, a thought,
a cold; gearboxes "in forty sizes" and papers carried from floor to floor; the glass in the kitchen; wells (the
potential well of 10); "not buried but swallowed"; the foundry with servers; tenants; "Electricity"; the column of
doublings; Achilles and the tortoise; the wall; the squirrel in a wheel; "That was a quotation. — In your voice"; the
old apple tree sawn up on doomsday; the steady trot; Genka's brother "who could sell snow in winter"; a photograph,
not a film; two bell towers; "To within the edge of the town."

**Facts used**: from the writer's memory, with web checks kept to the links and to the abstracts of five papers (at
the keeper's request); **check before reusing**. Checked on the day of writing (Crossref abstracts, Wikipedia):
Kleiber 1932 (Hilgardia 6: 315–353; the 3/4 power; BMR ≈ 3.4·w^0.75 W); Rubner 1883 (the surface law, respiration
trials on dogs); Bettencourt, Lobo, Helbing, Kühnert & West 2007 (PNAS 104: 7301; exponents of about 1.2 for wealth
creation and innovation and about 0.8 for infrastructure; the pace of social life increases with size; innovation
cycles "must be generated at a continually accelerating rate to sustain growth and avoid stagnation or collapse";
in the full text on PubMed Central: gasoline stations 0.77, length of electrical cables 0.87, total housing 1.00,
household water 1.01, serious crimes 1.16, new AIDS cases 1.23; walking speed rising with size; "an infinite
population in a finite amount of time", "the singularity is never reached", which Oleg reads out, and "finite-time
singularity" in Table 2; successive cycles that "reset the singularity", New York City as the example);
Daepp, Hamilton, West & Bettencourt 2015 (J. R. Soc. Interface: more than 25,000 publicly traded North American
companies, 1950–2009, an approximately constant hazard rate, a half-life of about a decade regardless of sector);
Arcaute et al. 2015 (J. R. Soc. Interface: England and Wales, thousands of boundary definitions, most indicators
linear, the exponent "fluctuates considerably"); Keuschnigg, Mutgan & Hedström 2019 (Science Advances: population
characteristics and selective migration explain around half of the superlinear scaling; that the data are Swedish
registers is from memory); von Foerster, Mora & Amiot 1960 (Science 132: 1291–1295, the title with the date; 13
November 2026 is a Friday). From memory, not checked: the pulse and lifespan figures (mouse 600 a minute and 2–3
years, elephant 30 and 60–70 years; "about a billion heartbeats" for most mammals; bats of mouse weight living thirty
years and more); Kleiber's animals "from a dove and a rat to a steer", his post at Davis, and the mouse and the
elephant added by others (Benedict, Brody); the arithmetic 200,000^(2/3) ≈ 3,400 and 200,000^(3/4) ≈ 9,500; West,
Brown & Enquist 1997 (Science 276: 122; the three assumptions) and 2001 (Nature 413: 628; one growth curve, the
guppy, the hen and the cow); the disputes over 2/3 and 3/4; Bornstein & Bornstein 1976 (Nature 259: 557; fifteen
towns, walking speed over a measured stretch); the exponents of the 2007 paper for road surface, jobs, wages, patents and research
employment, and "the United States, Germany and China"; "most of the dead companies were bought or merged, few went bankrupt" (the 2015
paper and West's *Scale*, 2017, which is also the only source for firms growing like animals); Rome's age; von
Foerster born in Vienna and working at Illinois, the fit holding for about another decade; world output growing by
about three percent a year; the largest yearly addition of 1989 (from 26). The column: with an exponent of 1.2 each
doubling takes 2^(−0.2) = 0.87 of the previous one, the sum is 40 / 0.129 ≈ 310 years; with 1.07 it is about 840
("eight hundred"). Links: the Russian Wikipedia has no pages for Kleiber and West (the English ones are used); the
English page "Luís Bettencourt" is a musician, the physicist is "Luís M. A. Bettencourt". Invented for the frame
(Oleg's side): the town of three hundred years, the plant built before the war and shut in 2007 that made gearboxes,
its shops now (a warehouse, a small firm of twenty, servers in the foundry), the miners' settlement, the junction,
the college and the market, Genka's brother, the House of Culture, Oleg's age of 62 (he left school in 1982). The
forum line about towns as the habitat of machines is a reader's (2016), paraphrased. Not used: Bettencourt 2013 (the
derivation of urban scaling); Kremer 1993; Jones 2022 on growth with a falling population (the argument is given in
Andrey's words; it belongs to 53); Detroit; the Fortune 500 turnover; Kapitsa.

**Figures**: `same-billion-heartbeats` (Oleg's count, round figures), `city-twice-as-large` (computed from the rounded
exponents), `three-kinds-of-growth` (a scheme of the growth equation on a logarithmic scale, example parameters).

**Left for later**: what becomes of the swallowed → 42 ("a separate evening"); whether inventions can come sooner
when the road to the edge of knowledge grows longer, and the growing number of heads put to inventing → 53 ("a
separate evening, and a serious one"; not developed); why what multiplies concentrates, and the sizes of cities → 49
(not touched); no row is given for the table of 46 (the facet is "law"); Oleg's next question, prompted by Genka's
reunion in the House of Culture: "Bring the seven-string tomorrow. What is it for at all?" → 41.

## 41. Cheesecake for the Ears

**Established**
- The answer to Oleg's "What is it for at all?" (40). Andrey brings the seven-string, plays an old waltz and fluffs
  one note. Oleg's question in the terms of the cycle: "evolution the miser" (33, 36) pays pleasure only against
  goods; "I am no fuller and no warmer, and a wolf could have walked up behind me. What was I paid for?" Darwin's
  "most mysterious" is given in one sentence; Pinker's "cheesecake for the ear" (1997) is given fairly, and Andrey
  agrees "by half": the buttons were made for something else, but Pinker "was in no hurry to ask which of them is the
  main one".
- The descent, on the guitar: seven notes of a scale and a stop ("it is like a sneeze that won't come"; "Who told
  you there was one more? — Nobody. It was owed"); the fir-tree song with the last note taken up ("Wrong", though "a
  bear trod on my ear"); the fluff in the unknown waltz that Oleg took for intended (a weak forecast, a cheap miss);
  a new song is noise the first time, hummed "to my shame" the fifth, and "a dripping tap" the five hundredth.
  **Guess nothing: noise; guess everything: a dripping tap; between is the good part.** Oleg: "I sit there and guess,
  all the time, without noticing."
- The lock with 03: mind acts ahead of events; "a hunter throws his spear at the place where the hare will be"; the
  miser should pay for a forecast that came true.
- The measurement: music is paid for in dopamine, "the same coin as for food" (the monkey and the juice of 18 is
  recalled, not retold); there are two payments, one at the favourite place and one before it, in the seconds of
  waiting, "from another drawer of the same till". **Stated precisely**: the gooseflesh itself falls on the place;
  the paying begins earlier. Gooseflesh is the reflex that raised an animal's fur ("Lofty. With the hackles up").
  The pill experiment shows that the dopamine is a cause and not a bystander.
- Oleg's objection from 36 ("there the head pays for a wrong guess caught, here for a right one; a cashier who pays
  for hits and misses alike pays for everything") is answered through the monkey: a forecast that cannot fail earns
  nothing, a miss from which nothing is learned earns nothing; what is paid is **a forecast that has just become
  better**. A joke trips once at the end; a tune trips gently a hundred times and brings home each time ("the
  confectioner of that evening sells one sweet; this one keeps a shop"). The chords are measured (sure and deceived,
  or unsure and given the likeliest); **that the pay is for a forecast getting better is said to be a hypothesis**
  ("I like it, which is not an argument"), as is Huron's reading that we pass the thanks for our own guess on to the
  sound.
- "A training machine: there is no hare, the pay is real, nothing is at risk" (a simulator, as play in 36 is the
  place where mistakes are cheap). **Whether it trains anything but the guessing of music is not claimed**: the
  Mozart effect did not survive repetition. "Pinker keeps his half: a sweet. I only say which button it presses. Not
  a side one, for sounds. The main one."
- The flutes: three times older than bread, and the ivory ones cost "days and days of a man's work in the Ice Age,
  on a thing that fills nobody's stomach" ("So somebody was paying even then. — Remember that too").
- Oleg's strongest objection: not cake but glue (the grandmother's lullaby, forty at a wedding table, a march, a
  choir, the barge haulers' "Yo, heave-ho"). Accepted as "stronger than the cake", with the measurements. Then the
  glue is taken apart: the song gives the haulers the moment, each knows it from the beat, that is, forecasts it.
  **A beat is a forecast made common: a hundred heads forecast the same instant and a hundred backs pull as one.**
  "Your glue is boiled from my button. — So we are both right. — The button is one, the uses are two." Not ours
  alone: the cockatoo.
- Where the glue went: Oleg remembers one verse of the table song, his granddaughter none; "thirty people in a bus,
  thirty pairs of earphones, thirty songs"; three guitars in the class and now none. "Who hauls barges now? — A tug.
  — Who sings on a tug? — The diesel." The song lived while a barge needed a hundred men. The author's 2017 forum
  posts are used in Andrey's mouth: the pit that needed a hundred diggers, the till with no cashier, **loneliness in
  a crowd**, and "the thought is not mine; I read it in Bill Joy". Sousa's 1906 fear of the phonograph. "The road
  from the choir to the earphones took a hundred and twenty years, and every step of it was a convenience. Nobody
  took the song away. We needed each other a little less each time." Oleg: "The cake is still there, and the glue
  has dried."
- The machine is brought in by Oleg, after the middle: Genka sent a song about their class (the names, the form
  mistress's nickname, the bench behind the boiler house), made by a program from three typed lines; Oleg had
  gooseflesh at the second verse and then felt he "had been handed a banknote and it turned out to be drawn". "But
  the hackles were real. — That is the annoying part."
- The author's 2009 line (machines will learn the laws of harmony and compose music better than people) and a
  reader's answer (good tools and laboratory mice, they will hardly write anything themselves), paraphrased without a
  name. **"Better" is not claimed**: nobody has measured it. What is given: the band that did not exist, the share
  of machine-made deliveries, their one to three plays in a hundred ("Machines sing, machines listen. Your lights-out
  production, only of songs"), and the blind test, with its caveat (ordered by a service that sells a detector).
- The shield "made by a human" (24) is not in the sound: between the second verse and Genka's laugh "not a note"
  changed; what changed is that "nobody had taken any trouble; there was nobody there". The fluff in the waltz
  ("fifty years of fingers") against a phone that fluffs ("broken"); the marathon runner and the bus ("nobody claps
  for a bus"); the granddaughter's card and the card with roses in gold. The author's 2025 line (a value is measured
  by the price people are ready to pay for it) is quoted as said "about quite other things" and extended "to small
  change": **in a song we value not only the sound but the price somebody paid for it.** The glue is of the same
  stuff: a lullaby and a choir are **receipts** (credible signalling; the dispute with the bonding camp is named);
  Darwin's peacock's tail in one line ("It was a seven-string tail. Few could appreciate it"). "A receipt that
  anybody can print is not a receipt."
- Oleg's second objection, in two parts, both accepted. (1) The gooseflesh came before he knew anything, so the
  sound works with no price, and the "price" is a label that can be dropped or faked. (2) The program's crack in the
  voice comes from ten thousand singers who paid in full. Andrey: (2) the price was paid once, by others, and is now
  copied for nothing (the lock with 21), which is why the song is no receipt, "a receipt is for what was paid now,
  by this one, for you"; (1) **there are two payments that used to come in one envelope**: for the sound (works
  whoever made it) and for the price (works only when one knows who paid). The first the machine makes no worse and
  will make better, measuring the dose "for one particular ear" (reward hacking, 22: "now it is not a confectioner
  who holds the button"). The second it cannot make, "and not for want of skill. It has nothing to pay with". What
  will be dear to the machine: **not known**; proof-of-work money is mentioned in one sentence and deferred (47).
- The shield stands "on two legs, and both are ours": one has to know who paid, and one has to need the person who
  paid; Oleg adds the third from 24 (the musician's listener has to earn). **Not claimed** that it will dry up
  altogether (full halls, people still pay to watch people play chess): "I am only saying what the shield is made
  of. — Of the same glue. — Of the same need."
- Anthropocentrism exposed ("music is the soul, a machine has none"): the task was named after our way, as with
  sleep and laughter. In training the machine does nothing but forecast what comes next, with notes as with words,
  "with no bonus: there is nobody to bribe" (36); whether anything is felt is the axiom of 20; whether it will have a
  beat to act as one or a receipt for a price depends on whether it has a pack and anything to pay with: **not
  known**. "What we called the soul of music turned out to be a button and a receipt. — And I had hackles. — The
  hackles were honest."
- Strengths stated: solid (the coin, the two payments, the pill; the chords; the flutes; people who kept time
  together share more); one survey by an interested party (the 97 in a hundred); argued about (cake, glue, tail or
  receipt: "I have taken a piece from each, which ought to put you on your guard"); a hypothesis (the pay is for a
  forecast getting better); Andrey's (the two payments are parting, and the second stands on our need of each other).
- Nobody says the forbidden word; ninth tick "again": "Came with a guitar. Came out at Genka's program, and brought
  it in myself."

**Images and devices used**: the seven-string bought because "seven is more than six" (the grandfather's
teach-yourself book, the mother's "now you can do what few can", the open strings that give a major chord); the
second entry in the notebook of dreams (an exam in strength of materials, in slippers; nobody rang); the scale that
stops on the seventh note; the sneeze that won't come; "It was owed"; the fir-tree song; a bear on the ear; noise and
a dripping tap; the hunter's spear; the till with two drawers; hackles; a pill for liking music; one sweet and a
shop; the muscle grown on the training machine; the split tusk; the wedding table of forty; "Yo, heave-ho"; "A parrot
in the choir"; "Oh frost, frost"; thirty pairs of earphones; the tug and the diesel; the tape recorder on the
windowsill; a drawn banknote; the bus nobody claps for; the sun with whiskers and the roses in gold; the receipt; the
seven-string tail; the envelope with two payments; two legs and a third; "a button and a receipt"; the closing
exchange, which is the plan's opening replica turned round ("Play something for the road. — What for? It won't fill
your stomach. — And why do you play? — You asked. — Is that the receipt? — The whole of it"), and the waltz played
again "with the same fluff".

**Facts used**: from the writer's memory, with web checks kept to two searches and to the links (at the keeper's
request); **check before reusing**. Checked on the day of writing: the titles, journals and years of the cited
papers, against Crossref (Salimpoor, Benovoy, Larcher, Dagher & Zatorre 2011, Nature Neuroscience,
10.1038/nn.2726; Ferreri et al. 2019, PNAS, 10.1073/pnas.1811878116; Cheung et al. 2019, Current Biology,
10.1016/j.cub.2019.09.067; Conard, Malina & Münzel 2009, Nature, 10.1038/nature08169; Mehr et al. 2019, Science,
10.1126/science.aax0868; Wiltermuth & Heath 2009, Psychological Science; Cohen, Ejsmond-Frey, Knight & Dunbar,
Biology Letters, online 2009; Patel, Iversen, Bregman & Schulz 2009, Current Biology; Rauscher, Shaw & Ky 1993,
